    Headline
    hepatomegaly, hepatotoxicity, lactic acidosis, steatosis
    General Information
    Lactic acidosis and severe hepatotoxicity, including fatal cases, have been reported with combination antiretroviral therapy which includes both nucleoside and nucleotide reverse transcriptase inhibitors. Typically, these adverse reactions present with symptoms such as enlargement of the liver (hepatomegaly) and fatty liver (steatosis), even in the absence of significant elevations in liver transaminases. Suspend efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate treatment in patients who develop clinical or laboratory findings suggestive of lactic acidosis or hepatotoxicity.

    Headline
    ataxia, dizziness, encephalopathy, hallucinations, headache, hypoesthesia, paresthesias, seizures, tinnitus, tremor, vertigo, visual impairment
    General Information
    Efavirenz has been associated with a range of neurologic adverse effects. The most commonly reported nervous system related adverse events in controlled trials involving antiretroviral-naive patients who received efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate included dizziness (3% to 35%) and headache (1% to 14%). Other adverse reactions reported during efavirenz controlled trials (including 1,008 efavirenz-treated patients) included dizziness (28.1%) and hallucinations (1.2%). Additional CNS side effects reported during postmarketing use of efavirenz-containing regimen include cerebellar coordination, abnormal coordination, ataxia, encephalopathy, convulsions or seizures, tremor, hypoesthesia, paresthesias, visual impairment (abnormal vision), tinnitus, and vertigo or balance disturbances. Symptoms usually begin on the first or second day of efavirenz therapy and resolve within 2 to 4 weeks; however, some patients may experience persistent CNS symptoms beyond 4 weeks. After 4 weeks of treatment, the prevalence of nervous system symptoms of at least moderate severity ranged from 5% to 9%. In some patients, late-onset neurotoxicity including ataxia and encephalopathy (i.e., impaired consciousness, confusion, psychomotor slowing, psychosis, delirium), may develop months to years after starting efavirenz. Of note, some cases of late-onset neurotoxicity have occurred in patients with CYP2B6 genetic polymorphisms which are associated with increased efavirenz concentrations despite standard dosing. Inform drug recipients that dosing during bedtime may improve tolerability, and that symptoms will likely improve with continued use and are not predictive of developing subsequent psychiatric symptoms. Instruct patients to avoid driving or operating machinery until they know how efavirenz may affect them and to avoid use of ethanol or other psychoactive drugs as these may worsen symptoms.

    Headline
    abnormal dreams, agitation, anxiety, depression, drowsiness, emotional lability, insomnia, mania, nightmares, paranoia, psychosis, suicidal ideation
    General Information
    The most commonly reported psychiatric related adverse events in controlled trials involving antiretroviral-naive patients who received efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate included abnormal dreams (2% to 11.3%), insomnia (3% to 6.5%), somnolence or drowsiness (3.1% to 3.9%), depression (3.1% to 11%), nightmares (1.9% to 2.6%), sleep disorder (1.3% to 2.2%), and anxiety (1.2% to 6%). Psychiatric related adverse events have been reported at a slightly higher frequency in patients receiving efavirenz 600 mg regimens compared with 400 mg regimens. Other serious psychiatric adverse reactions reported during efavirenz controlled trials (including 1,008 efavirenz-treated patients) included severe depression (2.4%), suicidal ideation (0.7%), nonfatal suicide attempts (0.5%), aggressive behavior (0.4%), paranoid reactions (i.e., paranoia or psychosis) (0.4%), manic reactions (i.e., mania) (0.2%), insomnia (16.3%), abnormal dreams (6.2%), drowsiness (7%), and impaired concentration (8.3%). There have also been postmarketing reports of death by suicide, delusions, catatonia, agitation, emotional lability, neurosis, and psychosis-like behavior.

    Headline
    alopecia, erythema multiforme, folliculitis, maculopapular rash, photosensitivity, pruritus, rash, skin ulcer, Stevens-Johnson syndrome, urticaria, vesicular rash
    General Information
    In efavirenz controlled trials, rash occurred in 26% of patients (n = 266 of 1,008) who received efavirenz in combination with other antiretroviral agents. Skin rash usually consists of mild-to-moderate maculopapular rash that occurs in the first 2 weeks of initiating therapy. In most cases, the rash resolves with continuing efavirenz therapy within 1 month. However, in 0.9% of patients (n = 9 of 1,008), the rash was associated with blistering, moist desquamation, and skin ulcer. Further, Grade 4 rashes (e.g., erythema multiforme, Stevens-Johnson syndrome) have been noted in 0.1% of efavirenz recipients (including patients from all studies and expanded access). A discontinuation rate for rash was 1.7% (n = 17 of 1,008) in efavirenz clinical trials. In controlled trials involving antiretroviral-naive patients who received efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate, dermatologic adverse events were observed in 9% to 32% of drug recipients. These dermatologic adverse events included maculopapular rash, erythematous rash, pruritic rash, macular rash, papular rash, morbilliform rash, vesicular rash, vesiculobullous rash, pustular rash, generalized rash, allergic dermatitis, eosinophilic pustular folliculitis, pruritus, and urticaria. During postmarketing experience, cases of photosensitivity (photoallergic dermatitis) and alopecia have been noted with use of efavirenz and lamivudine, respectively. Discontinue the use of efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate in patients with a rash associated with blistering, desquamation (e.g., exfoliative dermatitis), mucosal involvement, or pyrexa. The drug may be restarted following interruptions due to rash; however, for those patients experiencing a life-threatening cutaneous reaction (Stevens-Johnson syndrome), an alternative treatment is recommended. The use of appropriate antihistamines and corticosteroids may be considered when efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate is restarted; these agents may improve the tolerability and hasten the resolution of the rash.

    Headline
    teratogenesis
    General Information
    According to the manufacturer, retrospective case reports have described cases of teratogenesis (i.e., neural tube defects) in infants whose mothers were exposed to efavirenz-containing regimens during the first trimester of pregnancy. However, data from a meta-analysis of 23 studies found no increased risk of overall birth defects in infants born to mothers on efavirenz during the first trimester compared with those on other antiretroviral medications (relative risk 0.78; 95% CI, 0.56 to 1.08). Further, data from more than 7,900 periconception exposures to efavirenz in Botswana were sufficient to rule out an increased risk of neural tube defects with efavirenz.

    Headline
    flushing, palpitations, QT prolongation
    General Information
    Efavirenz 600 mg PO daily administered for 14 days to healthy subjects with CYP2B6 polymorphisms, specifically the CYP2B6 *6/*6 genotype, has resulted in a mean QT prolongation of 8.7 milliseconds. Other cardiovascular adverse events reported during postmarketing use of efavirenz include palpitations and flushing.

    Headline
    hypercholesterolemia, hyperlipidemia, hypertriglyceridemia
    General Information
    Hypercholesterolemia and hypertriglyceridemia were observed in patients treated with efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate during two clinical trials. In the first trial, 2% to 5% of the 630 drug recipients experienced Grade 3 to 4 increases in cholesterol concentration. Among the 299 drug recipients in the second trial, 19% had fasting cholesterol higher than 240 mg/dL and 1% had fasting triglycerides higher than 750 mg/dL. Monitor cholesterol and triglyceride concentrations before and periodically during treatment with efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate. If a patient develops hyperlipidemia during antiretroviral treatment, possible interventions include dietary modification, use of lipid lowering agents, or modification of the treatment regimen.

    Headline
    pancreatitis
    General Information
    Pancreatitis, which has been fatal in some cases, has been observed in antiretroviral-experienced pediatric patients receiving lamivudine alone and in combination with other antiretroviral agents. Cases of pancreatitis have also been observed during postmarketing use of tenofovir disoproxil fumarate. During efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate clinical trials, serum amylase concentrations greater than 175 units/L were observed in 9% of drug recipients.

    Headline
    breast enlargement, Cushingoid features, gynecomastia, lipodystrophy
    General Information
    While more commonly associated with protease inhibitor therapy, a lipodystrophy syndrome consisting of redistribution or accumulation of body fat including central obesity, dorsocervical fat enlargement (buffalo hump), peripheral wasting, facial wasting, breast enlargement, gynecomastia, and other cushingoid features has been reported in patients receiving long-term highly active antiretroviral therapy (HAART). The mechanism by which nucleoside analogues may cause body fat changes is not known. It has been suggested that nucleoside analogs may damage the mitochondria of adipocytes. An increased incidence of body fat changes is noted in those patients receiving long-term nucleoside therapy and in female patients. Lipodystrophy was observed in 1% of patients treated with efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate during clinical trials.

In addition, reports of constipation and malabsorption have been noted during postmarketing use of efavirenz.

    Headline
    arthralgia, back pain, elevated creatine kinase, myalgia, myopathy, rhabdomyolysis, weakness
    General Information
    Musculoskeletal adverse events reported by patients receiving efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate during clinical trials include arthralgia (5%), back pain (9%), generalized pain (13%), and myalgia (3%). In addition, 12% of drug recipients experienced elevated creatine kinase (Males: greater than 990 units/L; Females: greater than 845 units/L). During postmarketing use of efavirenz, cases of myopathy have been reported. Cases of rhabdomyolysis and muscle weakness have been noted during postmarketing use of lamivudine. Use of tenofovir disoproxil fumarate during the postmarketing period has been associated with rhabdomyolysis, muscle weakness, and myopathy.

    Headline
    autoimmune disease, Guillain-Barre syndrome, immune reconstitution syndrome
    General Information
    Immune reconstitution syndrome has been reported in patients treated with combination antiretroviral therapy. During the initial phase of HIV treatment, patients whose immune system responds to efavirenz therapy may develop an inflammatory response to indolent or residual opportunistic infections (such as progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy (PML), Mycobacterium avium complex (MAC), cytomegalovirus (CMV), Pneumocystis pneumonia (PCP), or tuberculosis (TB)), which may necessitate further evaluation and treatment. In addition, autoimmune disease (including Graves' disease, Guillain-Barre syndrome, autoimmune hepatitis, and polymyositis) may develop; the time to onset is variable and may occur months after treatment initiation.

    Headline
    neuritis, peripheral neuropathy
    General Information
    Patients with peripheral neuropathy receiving efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate can experience exacerbations during therapy due to the lamivudine component. In one case report involving a 57-year-old male with mild neuropathy, treatment with lamivudine was associated with a fatal peripheral neuropathy exacerbation. The patient had received lamivudine 300 mg daily for treatment of hepatitis. After 3 months of treatment, the patient presented with dysphonia and progressive muscle weakness. Later, the patient developed tetraparesis followed by acute respiratory failure requiring mechanical ventilation. The patient then went into sudden cardiac arrest. Treatment with lamivudine was stopped, and the neuropathy and respiratory capacity improved; however, the patient ultimately died. Peripheral neuropathy, which includes peripheral neuritis and neuropathy, was observed in 1% of patients receiving efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate during clinical trials.

    Headline
    laboratory test interference
    General Information
    Efavirenz does not bind to cannabinoid receptors. However, a laboratory test interference has been reported with some screening assays that resulted in false-positive urine cannabinoid test results in patients receiving efavirenz. Confirmation of positive screening tests for cannabinoids by a more specific assay is recommended.

Description
  Text
  Efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate is an oral combination product containing a non-nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitor (NNRTI), a nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitor (NRTI), and an acyclic nucleotide analog (i.e., nucleotide reverse transcriptase inhibitor). It is approved for use as a complete regimen to treat human immunodeficiency virus type 1 (HIV-1) infections in adult and pediatric patients weighing at least 35 kg (Symfi Lo with 400 mg efavirenz) or 40 kg (Symfi with 600 mg efavirenz). Although once recommended as a first-line treatment option for HIV-1, guidelines no longer recommend use of efavirenz-containing regimens due to the emergence of integrase strand transfer inhibitors (INSTIs), their low barrier to resistance, potential for drug interactions, and their tolerance profile. Additionally, similar to other NNRTI-containing regimens, it is not recommended to treat HIV-2 infections, as HIV-2 is intrinsically resistant to NNRTIs. Efavirenz is associated with several notable adverse reactions, including central nervous system symptoms (typically starting within the first two days of therapy and generally resolving after 2 to 4 weeks), psychiatric experiences (including severe depression, suicidal ideation, and aggressive behavior), QT prolongation, and skin rash (ranging from mild to severe, with rare cases of Stevens-Johnson syndrome). Additionally, efavirenz has been linked to cases of hepatitis and liver failure and is not recommended for patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment. Cases of new onset or worsening renal impairment have been associated with tenofovir and its use is not recommended for patients with a creatinine clearance less than 50 mL/minute. The FDA-approved labeling has a Black Box Warning for severe acute exacerbations of hepatitis B upon discontinuation of lamivudine or tenofovir in patients with both hepatitis B and HIV, which may occur with the combination product. Efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate was approved by the FDA in 2018.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  Efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate is active against infections caused by human immunodeficiency virus type 1 (HIV-1). Combination therapy targets different points in the life cycle of HIV, reducing the viral capacity to mutate into drug-resistant strains.
Efavirenz: Efavirenz is a non-nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitor (NNRTI). It binds directly and noncompetitively to a site on reverse transcriptase that is distinct from where nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors (NRTIs) bind. This binding induces a conformational change in the enzyme, disrupting its catalytic site and thereby inhibiting DNA polymerase activities. Unlike NRTIs, efavirenz does not compete with template or nucleoside triphosphates for incorporation into viral DNA and does not require phosphorylation to be active. By inhibiting reverse transcriptase, efavirenz interferes with the generation of DNA copies from viral RNA, which are essential for the synthesis of new virions.
Lamivudine: Lamivudine is a synthetic nucleoside analog of cytidine and a NRTI. Intracellularly, lamivudine is phosphorylated to produce the active 5'-triphosphate metabolite, lamivudine triphosphate (3TC-TP). The primary mode of action of 3TC-TP is inhibition of HIV-1 reverse transcriptase via viral DNA chain termination after incorporation of the nucleotide analog. Because the incorporated nucleoside analog lacks a 3'-hydroxyl group, it prevents the formation of the 5' to 3' phosphodiester linkage essential for DNA chain elongation, thereby inhibiting viral DNA growth.
Tenofovir disoproxil fumarate: Tenofovir disoproxil fumarate is an acyclic nucleoside phosphonate (nucleotide) diester analog of adenosine monophosphate. Tenofovir disoproxil fumarate requires initial diester hydrolysis for conversion to tenofovir. Tenofovir is then taken up by cells and undergoes phosphorylation to form tenofovir diphosphate (PMPApp). Tenofovir diphosphate inhibits HIV-1 reverse transcriptase by competing with the natural substrate deoxyadenosine 5'-triphosphate for incorporation into viral DNA. Because tenofovir diphosphate lacks a 3'-hydroxyl group, it causes chain termination by preventing the formation of the 5' to 3' phosphodiester linkage essential for DNA chain elongation. Tenofovir is a weak inhibitor of mammalian DNA polymerase alpha, beta, and mitochondrial DNA polymerase gamma.
 
Pharmacodynamics
Efavirenz: Efavirenz is virustatic rather than virucidal, meaning it suppresses viral replication but does not eliminate the virus from the body. Efavirenz exhibits antiviral activity against various HIV-1 subtypes; the 90% to 95% inhibitory concentrations (EC90 to 95) range from 1.7 to 25 nM.
Lamivudine: In cell cultures, 50% effective concentration (EC50) values for lamivudine ranged from 3 to 15,000 nM for HIV-1 and 3 to 120 nM for HIV-2 isolates.
Tenofovir: In cell culture studies, the EC50 of tenofovir for laboratory and clinical isolates of HIV-1 in lymphoblastoid cell lines, primary monocyte/macrophage cells, and peripheral blood lymphocytes ranged from 0.04 to 8.5 micromolar. Tenofovir displayed antiviral activity in cell cultures against HIV-1 clades A, B, C, D, E, F, G and O (EC50 values ranged from 0.5 to 2.2 micromolar) and showed strain-specific activity against HIV-2 (EC50 values ranged from 1.6 to 5.5 micromolar).
 
Resistance
Efavirenz: Resistance to efavirenz can develop rapidly, particularly when it is administered as part of an inadequately suppressive treatment regimen. The emergence of resistance is associated with specific mutations in the reverse transcriptase enzyme. In efavirenz resistant patients, mutations in reverse transcriptase occurred most frequently at amino acid position 103. Other reverse transcriptase mutations occurring at 1 or more amino acid positions 98, 100, 101, 106, 108, 188, 190, 225, 227, and 230 are also associated with resistance to efavirenz. Cross-resistance to NNRTIs has been observed. HIV-1 strains previously characterized as efavirenz-resistant were also phenotypically resistant to nevirapine and delavirdine. Avoid use of efavirenz in patients with HIV-2 infection, as HIV-2 is intrinsically resistant to NNRTIs. To identify the HIV strain, The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention guidelines for HIV diagnostic testing recommend initial HIV testing using an HIV-1 and HIV-2 antigen and antibody combination immunoassay and subsequent testing using an HIV-1 and HIV-2 antibody differentiation immunoassay.
Lamivudine: HIV-1 resistance to lamivudine is predominately due to methionine to valine or isoleucine (M184V/I) substitution in reverse transcriptase. Cross-resistance is expected with abacavir, didanosine, and emtricitabine.
Tenofovir: HIV-1 isolates that express K65R amino acid substitutions in reverse transcriptase show a 2- to 4-fold reduction in the susceptibility to tenofovir. In addition, a K70E substitution in HIV-1 reverse transcriptase has been selected by tenofovir and results in reduced susceptibility. The K70E substitution selected clinically by tenofovir results in reduced susceptibility to abacavir, didanosine, emtricitabine, and lamivudine. HIV-1 isolates with the K65R substitution also show reduced susceptibility to lamivudine and emtricitabine.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Administration of efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate with food has not been evaluated.
Efavirenz: Peak plasma concentrations are achieved 3 to 5 hours after administration of a single dose with steady-state plasma concentrations reached in 6 to 10 days.
Lamivudine: Following oral administration, lamivudine is rapidly absorbed with an absolute bioavailability of approximately 86%. Systemic drug exposure (AUC) and the maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) increase in proportion to the dose over a range from 0.25 to 10 mg/kg.
Tenofovir disoproxil fumarate: The bioavailability in a fasting state is 25%. Administration of tenofovir disoproxil fumarate after a high fat meal (700 to 1,000 kcal with 40% to 50% fat) increases the oral bioavailability, with an increase in AUC and Cmax of approximately 40% and 14%, respectively. Food delays the Cmax of tenofovir by approximately 1 hour.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    Efavirenz: No significant effects on efavirenz pharmacokinetics were observed in patients with mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A); data were insufficient to determine if moderate and severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B and C) impacted efavirenz pharmacokinetics.
Lamivudine: The pharmacokinetic of lamivudine are not significantly altered in patients with impaired hepatic function. Safety and efficacy have not been established in the presence of decompensated hepatic disease.
Tenofovir disoproxil fumarate: No substantial pharmacokinetic changes have been noted following tenofovir disoproxil fumarate administration in patients with moderate to severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B and C).
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    Efavirenz: Efavirenz pharmacokinetic parameters have not been studied in patients with renal impairment.
Lamivudine: As renal function decreases, lamivudine clearance decreases, leading to higher plasma concentrations (Cmax) and systemic exposure (AUC); however, the time to reach maximum concentration (Tmax) is not significantly affected by renal function. Hemodialysis increases lamivudine clearance; however, a 4-hour hemodialysis session did not significantly alter mean lamivudine exposure after a single dose. Continuous ambulatory peritoneal dialysis (CAPD) and automated peritoneal dialysis have negligible effects on clearance.
Tenofovir disoproxil fumarate: Tenofovir pharmacokinetics are altered in patients with renal impairment. A single dose study involving patients with varying degrees of renal dysfunction, found patients with CrCl less than 50 mL/minute or with end-stage renal disease requiring dialysis had increases in tenofovir exposure (AUC) and maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) when compared to patients with normal renal function.
    Name
    Gender Differences
    Description
    There are no significant or clinically relevant gender differences in the pharmacokinetics of efavirenz, lamivudine, or tenofovir disoproxil fumarate.
    Name
    Ethnic Differences
    Description
    Published literature has reported higher plasma concentrations in patients of African and Asian descent, as well as Latino patients, compared to Caucasian patients. Studies evaluating these racial differences attribute the elevated concentrations to single nucleotide polymorphisms (SNP) in CYP2B6, the enzyme primarily responsible for efavirenz metabolism. Patients with the SNP experience reduced CYP2B6 activity resulting in impaired efavirenz metabolism and elevated plasma concentrations. In a study evaluating the pharmacokinetics of efavirenz in 58 healthy subjects with CYP2B6 polymorphisms, the mean Cmax was found to be 2.25-fold higher in subjects with the CYP2B6 *6/*6 genotype than in those with the CYP2B6 *1/*1 genotype.
    Name
    Other
    Description
    Pregnancy
Efavirenz: Pregnancy does not significantly alter systemic concentrations (AUC) of efavirenz; however, the efavirenz geographic mean trough concentration (Cmin) is 24% lower during the third trimester as compared to postpartum (1.6 versus 2.05 mcg/mL; p = 0.01). Despite these lower values, the Cmin for most women (88%) still exceeds the minimum target concentration associated with virologic efficacy (1 mcg/mL). No dosage adjustments are necessary. There is moderate placental transfer to the fetus.
Lamivudine: Although population pharmacokinetic modeling suggests the oral clearance of lamivudine is increased by 22% during pregnancy, limited data from 2 studies involving 36 pregnant women (16 at 36 weeks, 20 at 38 weeks gestation) found the pharmacokinetics of lamivudine to be similar to those observed in non-pregnant and postpartum adults. No change in dose is indicated. In addition, placental transfer of lamivudine results in drug concentrations that are 2-times greater than maternal serum concentrations.
Tenofovir disoproxil fumarate: Pregnancy alters the pharmacokinetic parameters of tenofovir. In one study, a 30% increase in the clearance of tenofovir was observed in 34 HIV-infected pregnant women. In addition, the drug exposure (AUC), peak (Cmax), and trough (Cmin) obtained during the third trimester were 23% (p less than 0.001), 19% (p = 0.001) and 21% (p = 0.003) lower, respectively, than those obtained postpartum. Despite these reductions, most women maintain a therapeutic AUC of at least 2 mg x hr/L throughout pregnancy; thus, dosage adjustments are not required. Changes in the pharmacokinetics of tenofovir are theorized to be a result of increased renal clearance, expanded plasma volume, and reduced gastrointestinal absorption. Tenofovir has a high placental transfer to the fetus.
  Text
  Efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir is administered orally.
Efavirenz: Efavirenz is highly protein bound (greater than 99%), predominantly to albumin. The major metabolic pathways are via cytochrome P450 (CYP) 3A and CYP2B6 to hydroxylated metabolites, with subsequent glucuronidation to inactive metabolites. The terminal half-life is 52 to 76 hours after a single dose and 40 to 55 hours after multiple doses, as the drug induces its own metabolism. Approximately 14% to 34% of radiolabeled efavirenz or metabolites is excreted in the urine and 16% to 61% is eliminated in the feces. Nearly all the urinary excretion was in the form of metabolites, while efavirenz accounted for the majority of the total radioactivity measured in the feces.
Lamivudine: Once in systemic circulation, lamivudine is less than 36% bound to human plasma proteins. The apparent volume of distribution is 1.3 +/- 0.4 L/kg, indicating distribution into extravascular spaces. This volume of distribution is independent of dose and does not correlate with body weight. Metabolism is a minor elimination route, with approximately 5% of the dose being excreted in the urine as the trans-sulfoxide metabolite within 12 hours post-dose. The majority of the dose is eliminated unchanged in the urine by active organic cationic secretion. The observed mean elimination half-life ranges from 5 to 7 hours.
Tenofovir disoproxil fumarate: Protein binding of tenofovir is negligible (less than 0.7%) and is independent of plasma concentration. Intracellularly, tenofovir undergoes phosphorylation to its active metabolite, tenofovir diphosphate (PMPApp). In vitro studies indicate that neither tenofovir disoproxil fumarate nor tenofovir are substrates of cytochrome P450 enzymes. Tenofovir is eliminated by a combination of glomerular filtration and active renal tubular secretion; there may be competition for elimination with other compounds that are also renally eliminated. After intravenous administration, approximately 70% to 80% of the dose is recovered unchanged in the urine. The terminal elimination half-life after a single oral dose is approximately 17 hours.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 isoenzymes and transporters: CYP3A, CYP2B6, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, P-gp, BRCP
Efavirenz has been shown in vivo to induce CYP3A and CYP2B6. In vitro studies have shown efavirenz inhibits CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP3A4 with Ki values (8.5 to 17 microM) in the range of observed plasma concentrations. Efavirenz inhibited CYP2D6 and CYP1A2 only at concentrations well above those achieved clinically (Ki values 82 to 160 microM). Administration with drugs primarily metabolized by CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP3A, or CYP2B6 may result in altered plasma concentrations of the coadministered drug. Drugs that induce CYP3A or CYP2B6 activity are expected to increase the clearance of efavirenz. Lamivudine is an in vitro substrate of multidrug and toxic extrusion protein (MATE)1 and MATE2-K and organic cation transporter (OCT)2. Concurrent administration with an inhibitor of these drug transporters did increase lamivudine plasma concentrations; however, the increase was not considered clinically significant. Lamivudine is also a substrate of P-glycoprotein (P-gp) and breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP); however, its high bioavailability suggests minimal impact from efflux transporter inhibitors. Tenofovir disoproxil fumarate is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP transporters. Administration with inhibitors of these transporters may result in increased absorption of tenofovir.

Administration
  General Information
  For storage information, see the specific product information within the How Supplied section.
 
NOTE: Efavirenz; lamivudine; tenofovir disoproxil fumarate is a complete regimen. Do not administer with other antiretroviral medications.
NOTE: Interruptions of therapy may be necessary for specific situations.
If an unanticipated short-term treatment interruption (i.e., less than 1 to 2 days) is necessary, it is generally recommended that all antiretroviral agents be discontinued simultaneously, especially if the interruption occurs in a pregnant patient or is because of serious toxicity.
If a short-term treatment interruption is anticipated, as in the case of elective surgery, consider the pharmacokinetic properties and food requirements of the specific antiretroviral medications. Stopping a treatment regimen containing medications with differing half-lives may result in functional monotherapy of the antiretroviral with the longest half-life and may increase the risk for resistant mutations.
Health care providers are advised to restart a complete and effective antiretroviral regimen as soon as possible after a short-term interruption of therapy.
Planned long-term treatment interruptions are not recommended due to the potential for HIV disease progression (i.e., declining CD4 counts, viral rebound, acute viral syndrome), development of minor HIV-associated manifestations or serious non-AIDS complications, development of drug resistance, increased risk of HIV transmission, and increased risk for opportunistic infections.
If therapy must be discontinued, counsel the patient on the potential risks and closely monitor for any clinical or laboratory abnormalities.
  Route Specific Administrations
    Name
    Oral Administration
    Description
    Administer on an empty stomach.
Bedtime dosing is recommended to reduce nervous system side effects.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  No dosage adjustments are needed for patients with mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A). Not recommended for use in patients with moderate or severe impairment (Child-Pugh B or C) as there are insufficient data to determine if dosage adjustments are necessary.
  Renal Impairment
  CrCl 50 mL/minute or more: No dosage adjustment is needed.
CrCl less than 50 mL/minute: Not recommended.
 
Hemodialysis
Hemodialysis removes tenofovir from the blood, but is not expected to significantly remove efavirenz or lamivudine. Avoid use in patients with end-stage renal disease requiring hemodialysis.
